m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon)
following these steps. According to the above formula, we first need to compute
hM(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon)
. We have

h(M(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon))=\sum_{i=0}^{\min(2\bar{j},2j_{2}-1)}h_{j_{2}-\frac{i}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i}{2}}\lambda_{j_{2},\bar{j},i}C^{j_{2},\bar{j};j_{2}+\bar{j}-i}_{m_{2},\bar{m},m_{2}+\bar{m}}e^{(j_{2}-\frac{i+1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i-1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}}.

(4.36)

To compute the product
pM(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2}-\frac{i+1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i-1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}})
, we can use a variation of the formula
4.28
of
M^{-1}
to write
e^{(j_{2}-\frac{i+1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i-1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}}
as a polynomial

e^{(j_{2}-\frac{i+1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i-1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}}=\sum_{m^{\prime}}\lambda_{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2},i}^{-1}C^{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2};j_{2}+\bar{j}-i}_{m^{\prime},m_{2}+\bar{m}-m^{\prime};m_{2}+\bar{m}}e^{(j_{2}-\frac{1}{2})}_{m^{\prime}}\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}-m^{\prime}}.\\

(4.37)

Then we find that

\displaystyle pM(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}}

\displaystyle,e^{(j_{2}-\frac{i+1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{i-1}{2})}_{m_{2}+\bar{m}})=\sum_{m^{\prime}}(-1)^{2\bar{j}+1}\lambda_{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2},i}^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{2j_{2}(2j_{1}+1)(2\bar{j}+2)}{2j_{1}+2j_{2}}}

(4.38)

\displaystyle\times C^{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2};j_{2}+\bar{j}-i}_{m^{\prime},m_{2}+\bar{m}-m^{\prime};m_{2}+\bar{m}}C^{j_{1},j_{2}-\frac{1}{2};j_{1}+j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}_{m_{1},m^{\prime};m_{1}+m^{\prime}}C^{j_{1}+j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2};j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1}_{m_{1}+m^{\prime},m_{2}+\bar{m}-m^{\prime};m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}}e^{(j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1)}_{m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{2j_{1}+2j_{2}-i+1}\lambda_{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2},i}^{-1}\sqrt{2j_{2}(2j_{1}+1)(2\bar{j}+2)(2j_{2}+2\bar{j}-2i+1)}

\displaystyle\times C^{j_{2}+\bar{j}-i,j_{1};j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1}_{m_{2}+\bar{m},m_{1};m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}&\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2}&j_{2}+\bar{j}-i\\
j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1&j_{1}&j_{1}+j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\end{Bmatrix}e^{(j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1)}_{m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}},

where
\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&j_{3}\\
j_{4}&j_{5}&j_{6}\end{Bmatrix}
is the Wigner
6j
-Symbol.

Combining the above results, we find that
m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon)
is given by

\begin{aligned} m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},&\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon)=\sum_{i=0}^{\min(2\bar{j},2j_{2}-1)}(-1)^{2j_{1}+2j_{2}-i+1}\sqrt{\frac{(2j_{1}+1)2j_{2}(2j_{2}+1)(2j_{2}+2\bar{j}-2i+1)}{(2j_{2}-i)(2\bar{j}-i+1)}}\\
&\times C^{j_{2},\bar{j};j_{2}+\bar{j}-i}_{m_{2},\bar{m},m_{2}+\bar{m}}C^{j_{2}+\bar{j}-i,j_{1};j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1}_{m_{2}+\bar{m},m_{1};m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}&\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2}&j_{2}+\bar{j}-i\\
j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1&j_{1}&j_{1}+j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\end{Bmatrix}e^{(j_{1}+j_{2}-\bar{j}-1)}_{m_{1}+m_{2}+\bar{m}}\end{aligned}.

(4.39)

In fact, the above result is sufficient to determine all values of
m_{3}
. We have

\displaystyle m_{3}(\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon,e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}})

\displaystyle=-m_{3}(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon),

(4.40)

\displaystyle m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon,e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}})

\displaystyle=m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon)-m_{3}(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j})}_{\bar{m}}\epsilon).

(4.41)

Moreover, using the cyclic structure we have

\Tr_{S^{3}}\left(\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{2})}_{\bar{m}_{2}}\epsilon\times m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{\bar{m}_{1}}\epsilon)\right)=\Tr_{S^{3}}\left(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}}\times m_{3}(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{\bar{m}_{1}}\epsilon,\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{2})}_{\bar{m}_{2}}\epsilon)\right).

(4.42)

This determines the value of
m_{3}
on
H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})
.

For our later application, it will be more convenient to use the value of
m_{3}
in a different basis. We make the following change of variable

\begin{cases}&p=j_{1}+m_{1}\\
&q=j_{1}-m_{1}\end{cases},\quad\begin{cases}&r=j_{2}+m_{2}\\
&s=j_{2}-m_{2}\end{cases},\quad\begin{cases}&u_{1}=\bar{j}_{1}-\bar{m}_{1}\\
&v_{1}=\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{m}_{1}\end{cases},\quad\begin{cases}&u_{2}=\bar{j}_{2}-\bar{m}_{2}\\
&v_{2}=\bar{j}_{2}+\bar{m}_{2}\end{cases},

(4.43)

with the constraint that

u_{1}+u_{2}=p+r-1,\quad v_{1}+v_{2}=q+s-1.

(4.44)

This constraint is equivalent to
\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}=j_{1}+j_{2}-1
,
\bar{m}_{1}+\bar{m}_{2}=-(m_{1}+m_{2})
.
Then we define the constant
(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}
by the following

(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}:=\frac{(-1)^{\bar{j}_{1}-\bar{m}_{1}}N(j_{1},m_{1})N(j_{2},m_{2})}{N(\bar{j}_{1},\bar{m}_{1})N(\bar{j}_{2},\bar{m}_{2})}\Tr_{S^{3}}\left(\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{2})}_{\bar{m}_{2}}\epsilon\times m_{3}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{\bar{m}_{1}}\epsilon)\right).

(4.45)

where
N(j,m)=\sqrt{\frac{(j-m)!(j+m)!}{(2j+1)!}}
is the square root of the
S^{3}
norm of the monomial
w_{1}^{j+m}w_{2}^{j-m}
. This expression is non zero given the constrain
4.44
.

The constant
(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}
can be regarded as the value of
m_{3}
in a unnormalized basis. We have

m_{3}(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q},w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s},\frac{(u_{1}+v_{1}+1)!}{u_{1}!v_{1}!}\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{1}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{1}}\epsilon)=(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}w_{1}^{u_{2}}w_{2}^{v_{2}}.

(4.46)

Using the relation
4.40
and the cyclic property, we find that the constant
(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}
satisfy the following relation

(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1};u_{2},v_{2}}=-(m_{3})^{r,s;p,q}_{u_{2},v_{2};u_{1},v_{1}}.

(4.47)

Later we will build some interesting chiral OPE using this constant.

4.4
A_{\infty}
structure on the CR cohomology:
m_{n}

In this section, we analyze all the higher products
m_{n}
on the CR cohomology. First, by degree reason, the
n
-th product is only non zero on the following subspace of
H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\text{perm}}H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})^{\otimes 2}\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-2},

(4.48)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\text{perm}}H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-1}.

The fact that
H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
is concentrated in degree
0,1
and the homotopy operator
h
decrees the degree by
1
strongly restrict possible trees that contribute the higher product
m_{n}
. A tree that contains the following vertex must be zero
h
h
h\quad=0

As a result, for any tree that gives a non-zero map, all vertices must be directly connected to a leaf or the root. Moreover, the product map is zero on
\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})
. Therefore, for
n\geq 3
, a tree that gives a non-zero map must only consist of the following vertices:
i
H_{b}^{0,0}\;
i
\;H_{b}^{0,1}
h
h
i
\;H_{b}^{0,1}
h
p
h
h

Only a few trees survive under this condition.

First we consider the map
m_{n}
on
H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-1}
. In this case, only one tree contribute, which gives us

m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)=pM(hM(\dots hM(hM(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon),\bar{a}_{2}\epsilon),\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)),

(4.49)

for
a_{0}\in H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})
and
\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon\in H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})
.

We emphasize that the order of input elements does matter in a higher operation
m_{n}
. Therefore, the above formula does not directly apply to other cases when we insert
a_{0}\in H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})
in the middle. However, other cases can be computed using
m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)
and a combination of permutations of
\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon
. We have the following

m_{n}(\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{k+1}\epsilon,a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{k}\epsilon)=\sum_{\sigma\in Sh(k,n-1-k)}(\pm)m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{\sigma^{-1}(n-1)}\epsilon),

(4.50)

where
Sh(k,n-1-k)
the subset of
(k,n-1-k)
-shuffles in
S_{n-1}
. We see that it suffice to only compute
m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)
to determine the value of
m_{n}
on
\bigoplus_{\text{perm}}H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-1}
.

For
n\geq 3
, let us denote

\mu_{n}(a_{0};\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)=M(hM(\dots hM(hM(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon),\bar{a}_{2}\epsilon),\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)).

(4.51)

Then
m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)=p\mu_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)
. The purpose of defining
\mu_{n}
is that it can be computed iteratively as follows

\mu_{n}(a_{0};\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)=M(h\mu_{n-1}(a_{0};\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-2}\epsilon),\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon).

(4.52)

Computation of
\mu_{n}
is similar as the computation of
m_{3}
in the last section. First we compute
\mu_{3}(e^{(j_{0})}_{m_{0}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{2})}_{m_{2}}\epsilon)
. Using
D.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{3}(e^{(j_{0})}_{m_{0}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{2})}_{m_{2}}\epsilon)

(4.53)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}h_{j_{0}-\frac{i_{1}}{2},\bar{j}_{1}-\frac{i_{1}}{2}}\lambda_{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1},i_{1}}\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}_{1}+\frac{1}{2};i_{1}}^{-1}\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}+\frac{1}{2};i_{2}}\sqrt{(2\bar{j}_{1}+2)(2\bar{j}_{2}+1)(2j_{0}+2\bar{j}_{1}-2i_{1}+1)}

\displaystyle\times\begin{Bmatrix}\bar{j}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}&j_{0}-\frac{1}{2}&j_{0}+\bar{j_{1}}-i_{1}\\
j_{0}+\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}-i_{2}&\bar{j}_{2}&\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\end{Bmatrix}C^{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1};j_{0}+\bar{j}_{1}-i_{1}}_{m_{0},m_{1};m_{0}+m_{1}}C^{j_{0}+\bar{j}_{1}-i_{1},\bar{j}_{2};j_{0}+\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}-i_{0}}_{m_{0}+m_{1},m_{2};m_{0}+m_{1}+m_{2}}e^{(j_{0}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i_{2}}{2},\bar{j}_{1}+\bar{j}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i_{2}}{2})}_{m_{0}+m_{1}+m_{2}}.

In general,
\mu_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon)
takes the following form

\mu_{n}(e^{(j_{0})}_{m_{0}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\dots\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-1})}_{m_{n-1}}\epsilon)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}(\mu_{n})^{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1}\dots,\bar{j}_{n-1}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\left(\prod_{l=0}^{n-2}C^{J_{l}-i_{l},\bar{j}_{l+1},J_{l+1}-i_{l+1}}_{M_{l},m_{l+1},M_{l+1}}\right)e^{(j_{0}-\frac{n-2}{2}-\frac{i_{n-1}}{2},\bar{J}_{n-1}+\frac{n-2}{2}-\frac{i_{n-1}}{2})}_{M_{n-1}}\epsilon,

(4.54)

where
i_{0}=0
and we define

\displaystyle J_{l}=j_{0}+\bar{j}_{1}+\dots+\bar{j}_{l},\quad l\geq 0,

(4.55)

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{l}=J_{l}-j_{0},\quad l\geq 1,

\displaystyle M_{l}=m_{0}+\dots+m_{l},\quad l\geq 0.

Using the recursion relation
4.52
and the formula
D.7
, we find that

\displaystyle(\mu_{n})^{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1}\dots,\bar{j}_{n-1}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=2}^{n-1}h_{j_{0}-\frac{l-2}{2}-\frac{i_{l-1}}{2},\bar{J}_{l-1}+\frac{l-2}{2}-\frac{i_{l-1}}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bar{J}_{l-1}+l)(2\bar{j}_{l}+1)(2J_{l-1}-2i_{l-1}+1)}

(4.56)

\displaystyle\times\left(\prod_{l=1}^{n-2}\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{l-1}{2},\bar{J}_{l}+\frac{l-1}{2};i_{l}}\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{l}{2},\bar{J}_{l}+\frac{l}{2};i_{l}}^{-1}\right)\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{n-2}{2},\bar{J}_{n-1}+\frac{n-2}{2};i_{n-1}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=2}^{n-1}\begin{Bmatrix}\bar{J}_{l-1}+\frac{l_{1}}{2}&j_{0}-\frac{l-1}{2}&J_{l-1}-i_{l-1}\\
J_{l}-i_{l}&\bar{j}_{l}&\bar{J}_{l}+\frac{l-1}{2}\end{Bmatrix}.

The above expression simplifies to

\displaystyle(\mu_{n})^{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1}\dots,\bar{j}_{n-1}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}=

(4.57)

\displaystyle\lambda_{j_{0}-\frac{n-2}{2},\bar{J}_{n-1}+\frac{n-2}{2};i_{n-1}}\prod_{l=2}^{n-1}\sqrt{\frac{(2j_{0}-l+3)(2\bar{j}_{l}+1)(2J_{l-1}-2i_{l-1}+1)}{(2j_{0}-l+2-i_{l-1})(2\bar{J}_{l-1}+l-1-i_{l-1})}}\begin{Bmatrix}\bar{J}_{l-1}+\frac{l-1}{2}&j_{0}-\frac{l-1}{2}&J_{l-1}-i_{l-1}\\
J_{l}-i_{l}&\bar{j}_{l}&\bar{J}_{l}+\frac{l-1}{2}\end{Bmatrix}.

As a result, we have the following formula for
m_{n}(e^{(j_{0})}_{m_{0}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\dots\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-1})}_{m_{n-1}}\epsilon)

m_{n}(e^{(j_{0})}_{m_{0}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\dots\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-1})}_{m_{n-1}}\epsilon)=\left.\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-2}}(\mu_{n})^{j_{0},\bar{j}_{1}\dots,\bar{j}_{n-1}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\left(\prod_{l=0}^{n-2}C^{J_{l}-i_{l},\bar{j}_{l+1},J_{l+1}-i_{l+1}}_{M_{l},m_{l+1},M_{l+1}}\right)\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{0}=0;\\
i_{n-1}=2j_{0}-n+2\end{subarray}}\bar{e}^{(\bar{J}_{n-1}-j_{0}+n-2)}_{M_{n-1}}\epsilon.

(4.58)

Here, the range of summation is taken to be

0\leq i_{l}\leq\min\{2j_{0}-l,2\bar{J}_{l}+l-1\},\;\text{ for }l=1,\dots n-2.

(4.59)

However, the actual range of summation is much smaller due to the constraint of the Wigner
6j
symbol and the Clebsch–Gordan coefficients. For example, the summand is nonzero only when

i_{l}\geq i_{l-1},\;\text{ for }l=2,\dots n-1.

(4.60)

Using the cyclic structure, the above result is sufficient to determine the whole
m_{n}
. For example, we have the following

\Tr_{S^{3}}\left(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}}\times m_{n}(e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\dots\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-1})}_{m_{n-1}}\epsilon)\right)=\Tr_{S^{3}}\left(\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-1})}_{m_{n-1}}\epsilon\times m_{n}(e^{(j_{1})}_{m_{1}},e^{(j_{2})}_{m_{2}},\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{1})}_{m_{1}}\epsilon,\dots\bar{e}^{(\bar{j}_{n-2})}_{m_{n-2}}\epsilon)\right)

(4.61)

This determines the value of
m_{n}
on
H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})^{\otimes 2}\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-2}
. Other values of
m_{n}
can be determined similarly.

4.5
L_{\infty}
structure on the tensor product

In this section, we study the
L_{\infty}
structure on the tensor product between the
C_{\infty}
algebra
H^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}_{b}(S^{3})
and a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. It was shown in
[
34
]
that for an arbitrary
C_{\infty}
algebra and a Lie algebra, there exists a canonical
L_{\infty}
structure on the tensor product. However, an explicit formula is not provided in
loc. cit.
due to the fact that the operad
\mathcal{L}ie
does not have a canonical basis.

In our cases, an explicit formula can be easily obtained by thinking about the homotopy transfer of the Lie algebra
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}
. The homotopy transfer from a Lie algebra to a
L_{\infty}
algebra is similar to the homotopy transfer of associative algebra, except that we consider all (not necessarily planar) binary rooted trees and we replace the product map with the Lie bracket on each vertex. In our cases, the condition on non zero trees discussed in the last section still hold. Therefore, up to a permutation of the leaves, the trees that contribute to the
L_{\infty}
operations are the same as in the last section. As a result, we have the following formula for the higher bracket
l_{n}
restricted on
(H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g})\bigwedge^{n-1}(H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g})

\displaystyle l_{n}(a_{0}\otimes x_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon\otimes x_{1},...,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon\otimes x_{n-1})

(4.62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n-1}}m_{n}(a_{0},\bar{a}_{\sigma(1)}\epsilon,...,\bar{a}_{\sigma(n-1)}\epsilon)[...[[x_{0},x_{\sigma(1)}],x_{\sigma(2)}],...,x_{\sigma(n-1)}],

where
a_{0}\in(H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3}),\bar{a}_{i}\epsilon\in H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})
and
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
. We have that
|S_{n-1}|=(n-1)!=\dim\mathcal{L}ie(n)
. In fact, the elements
[...[[x_{0},x_{\sigma(1)}],x_{\sigma(2)}],...,x_{\sigma(n-1)}],]
,
\sigma\in S_{n-1}
form a basis of the Lie operad
\mathcal{L}ie(n)
and are called Dynkin elements.

Using the above
L_{\infty}
structure, one can write down the full effective interaction of the KK theory. Recall that we denote
\{t_{a}\}
a basis of the Lie algebra. We let
K:\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{C}
the Killing form and denote
K_{ab}=K(t_{a},t_{b})
. We define
t^{a}=\sum_{b}K^{ab}t_{b}
where
K^{ab}
is the inverse of
K_{ab}
. The KK fields are expanded as
\mathbf{A}=\sum_{p,q\geq 0}\sum_{a}\mathbf{A}^{a}[p,q]t_{a}w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}
and
\mathbf{B}=\sum_{p,q\geq 0}\sum_{a}\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}\mathbf{B}_{a}[p,q]t^{a}\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}

Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(n-1)!}\sum_{n\geq 2}\sum_{p,q,r,s\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1}+\dots u_{n-1}=p+r-n+2\\
v_{1}+\dots v_{n-1}=q+s-n+2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\\
c_{1},\dots,c_{n-1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n-1}}

(4.63)

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{(u_{i}+v_{i}+1)!}{u_{i}!v_{i}!}\right)\Tr_{S^{3}}(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\times m_{n}(w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s},\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{\sigma(1)}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{\sigma(1)}}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{\sigma(n-1)}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{\sigma(n-1)}}\epsilon))

\displaystyle\times K(t_{a},[...[[t_{b},t^{c_{\sigma(1)}}],t^{c_{\sigma(2)}}],...,t^{c_{\sigma(n-1)}}])\int\mathbf{A}^{a}[p,q]\mathbf{A}^{b}[r,s]\mathbf{B}_{c_{1}}[u_{1},v_{1}]\cdots\mathbf{B}_{c_{n-1}}[u_{n-1},v_{n-1}],

where
m_{n}
can be computed by formula
4.58
. This gives us a complete answer to the classical action functional of the KK theory.

5
Holomorphic BF theory

5.1
A brief review

In this section, we introduce the holomorphic BF theory. Fields of holomorphic BF theory are

\displaystyle{\EuScript\displaystyle A}\in\Omega^{0,1}(X,\mathfrak{g})[1],

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\EuScript\displaystyle B}\in\Omega^{3,1}(X,\mathfrak{g})[1].

The action functional is given by

S=\int_{X}\EuScript{B}(\bar{\partial}\EuScript{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\EuScript{A},\EuScript{A}]).

(5.2)

By varying the action function we obtain the following equations of motion.

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\EuScript{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\EuScript{A},\EuScript{A}]=0,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\EuScript{B}+[\EuScript{A},\EuScript{B}]=0.

The gauge transformations are generated by
\chi\in\Omega^{0,0}(X,\mathfrak{g}),\nu\in\Omega^{3,0}(X,\mathfrak{g})
with

\displaystyle\delta\EuScript{A}=\bar{\partial}\chi+[\EuScript{A},\chi],

(5.4)

\displaystyle\delta\EuScript{B}=\bar{\partial}\nu+[\EuScript{B},\nu].

For the purpose of performing KK reduction, it is more convenient to reformulate the theory into BV formalism. By adding ghosts, anti-fields and anti-ghost, the BV field content can be described by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}\in\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X,\mathfrak{g})[1],

(5.5)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\EuScript{B}}\in\Omega^{3,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X,\mathfrak{g})[1].

The BV action functional is given by

HBF[\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}},\boldsymbol{\EuScript{B}}]=\int_{X}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{B}}(\bar{\partial}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}+\frac{1}{2}[\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}},\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}]).

(5.6)

We see that the BV theory is encoded in the following dg Lie algebra

(\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X,\mathfrak{g})\oplus\Omega^{3,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X,\mathfrak{g}),\bar{\partial},[-,-]).

(5.7)

5.2
Cohomological KK reduction

In this section, we study KK reduction of holomorphic BF theory on
\mathbb{C}^{3}/\mathbb{C}
.

Since fields of holomorphic BF theory are also Dolbeault forms, we can use the same results as we analyze the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory to perform the KK reduction. We work in the BV formalism, decompose the field content into
S^{3}
harmonics and then pass to the CR cohomology. The field
\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}
leads to the following KK tower of fields
4
4

4

To avoid introducing too many symbols, we used the same letter
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
here as in the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. The correct meaning of the symbol should be clear from the context.

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}[1],

(5.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}.

We can choose the standard holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=dzdz_{1}dz_{2}
.Then the field
\boldsymbol{\EuScript{B}}
leads to the following KK tower of fields

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},

(5.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}[1].

Remark 5.1
.

More precisely, the KK fields corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\EuScript{B}}
live in
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},(1)}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{2,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}
\oplus\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},(1)}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{2,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}[1]
. By choosing the volume form, we can forget the difference in flat space.

We can replace
\epsilon
by
dz
and identify
\mathbb{C}[\bar{w}_{1},\bar{w}_{2}]
with the dual of
\mathbb{C}[w_{1},w_{2}]
as in Section
3.5
. According to our discussion in Section
3.5
, this field content is exactly the holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory with a vector multiplet valued in
\mathfrak{g}[w_{1},w_{2}]
and an adjoint chiral multiplet.

Having the field content, the next step is to analyze the action functional. The information about the action functional is encoded in the
L_{\infty}
structure on the BV fields and the integration map. With the choice of holomorphic volume form, the integration map is the same as the integration map we studied in holomorphic Chern-Simons theory
3.2
. The dg Lie algebra of the holomorphic BF theory can be identified with the shifted cotangent structure of
(\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X,\mathfrak{g}),\bar{\partial},[-,-])
, which encode holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. As a result, we don’t need to go through the process of homotopy transfer again. After we pass to the CR cohomology, the
L_{\infty}
structure is given by the shifted cotangent structure of the
L_{\infty}
algebra
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g},\{l_{k}\})
. We identify the dual of the Lie algebra with itself via the Killing pairing. In summary, the
L_{\infty}
structure corresponding to the KK reduction of holomorphic BF theory consists of the following data

1.

the
L_{\infty}
algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{CR}=(H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g},\{l_{k}\})
.

2.

A copy
\mathfrak{g}_{CR}^{\prime}
of
H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}
considered as an abelian Lie algebra.

3.

An
\mathfrak{g}_{CR}
module structure on
\mathfrak{g}_{CR}^{\prime}
. This
L_{\infty}
module structure is given by maps

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{CR}^{\otimes n-1}\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{CR}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{g}_{CR},

(5.10)

\displaystyle(t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1},t^{\prime}_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto l_{n}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1},t^{\prime}_{n}),

where
t_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{CR}
and
t_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}_{CR}^{\prime}
.

As a consequence, we can build up the equation of motion and action functional for the KK theory of holomorphic BF theory by using the same set of data
\{l_{k}\}
as in the last section. Using the differential and the
2
-bracket, we get the following action functional

\int_{3d}\Tr\left(\mathbf{B}(\hat{d}\mathbf{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}])+\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(\hat{d}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+[\mathbf{A},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}])\right),

(5.11)

which coincide with the action functional of HT twist of
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet with an adjoint chiral multiplet
3.64
.
Higher brackets lead to the following interactions

\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\int\frac{1}{2(n-2)!}\Tr(\mathbf{B}\wedge l_{n}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A},\underbrace{\tilde{\mathbf{B}},\dots,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}_{n-2}))+\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\Tr(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\wedge l_{n}(\mathbf{A},\underbrace{\tilde{\mathbf{B}},\dots,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}_{n-1})).

(5.12)

In summary, we have the following

Proposition 5.1
.

The KK reduction of the holomorphic BF theory on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the same as the holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory, associated to a
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet valued in
\mathfrak{g}[w_{1},w_{2}]
and an adjoint chiral multiplet. The action functional consist of the standard holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
action, together with the deformation
5.12
.

The full action functional can be written down explicitly using the higher product
m_{n}
we computed in Section
4.4
, and is similar to the expansion of holomorphic Chern-Simons
4.63
.

5.3
Residue supersymmetry and its breaking

According to the discussion of the last section, the zero modes of KK theory of holomorphic BF theory gives rise to the holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory with a vector multiplet and an adjoint chiral multiplet. In fact, this field content consists of a
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet. A
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
theory has more supersymmetry than
\mathcal{N}=2
theory, and has residue supersymmetry that survives after the
holomorphic twist. This residue supersymmetry and be used to further twist the theory, and will lead to the
A
and
B
topological twists of
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
theory. Details of the relation between holomorphic twist and the
A
and
B
twist are studied in
[
38
]
. Here we briefly review some of the results.

The theory formulated using the CR cohomology has an infinite series of interactions, which is cumbersome for the analysis of symmetry. Therefore, we use the equivalent formulation of the KK theory built on the CR cochain. The BV fields consist of

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}[1],

(5.13)

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g},

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{B}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}[1].

The action functional of this theory contains the standard action functional
3.64
for a
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet, together with the following deformation

\int(\bm{\Psi}\bar{\partial}_{b}\bm{\mathcal{A}}).

(5.14)

For the
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet, with the standard holomorphic twisted action
S_{HT}
,
[
38
]
identified the deformation corresponding to the further
A
and
B
topological twist as follows

\delta S_{A}=\int\bm{\mathcal{B}}\bm{\Phi},\quad\quad\delta S_{B}=\int\bm{\Psi}\partial_{z}\bm{\mathcal{A}}.

(5.15)

These two deformations are compatible with the standard holomorphic twisted action
S_{HT}

\{\delta S_{A},S_{HT}\}=\{\delta S_{B},S_{HT}\}=0.

(5.16)

They also generate the residue supersymmetry. Let
Q_{A}=\{\delta S_{A},-\}
and
Q_{B}=\{\delta S_{B},-\}
, we have

\{Q_{A},Q_{B}\}=\partial_{z}.

(5.17)

For theory with the standard HT twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
action, we can add either
Q_{A}
or
Q_{B}
to the BRST differential, or equivalently add
\delta S_{A}
or
\delta S_{B}
to the BV action functional. Then
\partial_{z}
will become BRST exact, and the new theory will become topological. They correspond to the
A
and
B
topological twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=4
theory respectively.

However, adding the deformed term
5.14
will break the residue supersymmetry. We have

\{\int(\bm{\Psi}\bar{\partial}_{b}\bm{\mathcal{A}}),\delta S_{A}\}=\int\bm{\Psi}\bar{\partial}_{b}\bm{\Phi}+\int(\bm{\mathcal{B}}\bar{\partial}_{b}\bm{\mathcal{A}})\neq 0.

(5.18)

As a consequence, the residue supercharge
Q_{A}
does not survive the interaction of the KK modes.

Although the residue supersymmetry is broken, the other supercharge
Q_{B}
, is still compatible with the full KK theory.

\{\int(\bm{\Psi}\bar{\partial}_{b}\bm{\mathcal{A}}),\delta S_{B}\}=0.

(5.19)

This means that we can still perform a further ”
B
twist” by adding
Q_{B}
to the BRST differential. This has the effect of turning the differential
\hat{d}
into
\hat{d}+\epsilon\partial_{z}
. However, this ”
B
twist” will not lead to a topological theory as the
\partial_{z}
is no longer BRST exact
5
5

5

This deformation is topological only for the zero mode
. It is interesting that this further ”
B
twist” can also be accomplished by putting the
6d
theory on the deformed geometry
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. We will see this in Section
8
.

6
Poisson BF theory

6.1
A brief review

Holomorphic Poisson BF theory, or Poisson BF theory for short, is the same as an abelian BF theory together with a Poisson bracket. The field content of Poisson BF theory consist

\displaystyle{\EuScript\displaystyle H}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C}^{3}),

(6.1)

\displaystyle{\EuScript\displaystyle W}\in\Omega^{3,1}(\mathbb{C}^{3}).

We introduce the following holomorphic Poisson tensor

\pi=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{2}}.

(6.2)

It induces the Poisson bracket

\{f,g\}_{\pi}=\epsilon_{ij}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial z_{j}}.

(6.3)

Using the Poisson bracket, the action functional can be written as

\int\EuScript{W}(\bar{\partial}\EuScript{H}+\frac{1}{2}\{\EuScript{H},\EuScript{H}\}_{\pi}).

(6.4)

Like holomorphic BF theory, Poisson BF theory has gauge transformations generated by
\chi\in\Omega^{0,0}(X,\mathfrak{g}),\nu\in\Omega^{3,0}(X,\mathfrak{g})
. It takes the following form

\displaystyle\delta\EuScript{H}=\bar{\partial}\chi+\{\EuScript{H},\chi\}_{\pi},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\delta\EuScript{W}=\bar{\partial}\nu+\{\EuScript{W},\nu\}_{\pi}.

Remark 6.1
.

For Poisson BF theory on twistor space
\mathbb{PT}
, the fields should be
\EuScript{H}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{PT},\mathcal{O}(2))
,
\EuScript{W}\in\Omega^{3,1}(\mathbb{PT},\mathcal{O}(-2))
. However, we will be working with the theory on a open patch
\mathbb{C}^{3}\subset\mathbb{PT}
, so we can forget the difference o the line bundle.

It will be convenient to reformulate the theory into BV formalism. The BV field content consists of

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\EuScript{H}}\in\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\EuScript{W}}\in\Omega^{3,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}).

The BV action functional is given by

\int\boldsymbol{\EuScript{W}}(\bar{\partial}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{H}}+\frac{1}{2}\{\boldsymbol{\EuScript{H}},\boldsymbol{\EuScript{H}}\}_{\pi}).

(6.7)

Note that the BV structure of the theory is encoded in the following dg Lie algebra structure

(\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3})\oplus\Omega^{3,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}),\bar{\partial},\{-,-\}_{\pi}),

(6.8)

6.2
Cohomological KK reduction

In this section, we analyze the KK reduction of Poisson BF theory, specifically the KK theory built on the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology. Note that the dg Lie algebra structure encoding Poisson BF theory is the same as the (shifted) cotangent structure on the following dg Lie algebra

(\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}),\bar{\partial},\{-,-\}_{\pi}).

(6.9)

Therefore, we only need to understand how the Lie bracket
\{-,-\}_{\pi}
on
\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
behave under the KK reduction. As a first step, we use the isomorphism
K
to identify the complex with
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+\bar{\partial}_{b})
. The Poisson bracket
\{-,-\}_{\pi}
(which is a Lie bracket) induces a dg Lie algebra structure on the space
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. Therefore, we have the following isomorphic dg Lie algebra:

(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\pi}).

(6.10)

Note that the Lie bracket above is defined as
K(\{K^{-1}(\alpha),K^{-1}(\beta)\}_{\pi})
. To simplify the notation we still denote it by
\{-,-\}_{\pi}
.
Unlike the KK reduction of holomorphic Chern-Simons, where the product structure comes separately from the product on CR complex and the product on differential forms
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
, the induced bracket
\{-,-\}_{\pi}
contains mixing term between the two complexes. For example, we compute the bracket of
t^{2}
and
w_{1}
. The images of
t^{2}
and
w_{1}
under the map
K^{-1}
are
z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{2}
and
z_{1}
respectively. We have
\{(z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{2}),z_{1}\}_{\pi}=-\bar{z}_{2}
. This implies

\{t^{2},w_{1}\}_{\pi}=-t^{2}\bar{w}_{2}.

(6.11)

Terms like this will lead to a complicated form of the Lie brackets
\{-,-\}_{\pi}
on the complex
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
, and will result in a cumbersome action functional. Passing to the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology will make things even worse. Computing the higher brackets on the
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
will be an elaborate job.

One can actually simplifies the Lie bracket on
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. Instead of passing to the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology, we first pass to the cohomology of
\hat{d}
. The cohomology of
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}),\hat{d})
is simple and gives us
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}
, which is the space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}
. Since the cohomology is concentrated in degree
0
, no higher bracket is generated at this step. Therefore, we find the following complex that is quasi-isomorphic to the complex
6.10

(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}}).

(6.12)

By restricting the Poisson bracket to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
, we find that there is no mixing term in the bracket
\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}}
between
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})

\{f(z),Y(w_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{\bar{\pi}}=0.

(6.13)

Therefore, this bracket can be defined solely on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
, and we have the dg Lie algebra
(\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
. In this way, the dg Lie algebra
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
is the tensor product of the commutative algebra
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}
with the dg Lie algebra
(\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
.

Then we can pass to the CR cohomology. Applying homotopy transfer theorem the dg Lie algebra
(\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
, we obtain an
L_{\infty}
algebra

(H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),0,\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}},\{-,-,-\}_{\bar{\pi},3},\dots).

(6.14)

If we tensor the above
L_{\infty}
algebra with the dg commutative algebra
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}},\hat{d})
, we obtain a
L_{\infty}
algebra

(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d},\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n},n\geq 2).

(6.15)

Passing to the
\hat{d}
cohomology, we get an
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphic
L_{\infty}
algebra
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n},\;n\geq 2)
, which is further
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphic to
6.12
.

To summary, we have the following chain of
L_{\infty}
-quasi isomorphism

(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+\bar{\partial}_{b},\{-,-\}_{\pi})\overset{i}{\leftarrow}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n})\overset{i}{\rightarrow}(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n}).

(6.16)

As a consequence, we can equivalently work with the
L_{\infty}
algebra
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n})
for our field theory. The difference between this
L_{\infty}
structure and the original structure
6.10
is that this
L_{\infty}
structure is defined completely on the tangential Cauchy Riemann cohomology
H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. Analysis of this field theory can be done under the same framework of twisted
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory.

If we omit all the higher brackets
\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n}
,
n\geq 3
, the Lie algebra
(H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
can be identified with

T^{*}[1]\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),

(6.17)

where
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
is the Lie algebra of Hamiltonian vector fields on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. We provide more detail about this fact in the next section.

Using the special deformation retract constructed in
4.1
, the transferred
L_{\infty}
structure is a cyclic
L_{\infty}
algebra with the symmetric bilinear pairing given by
\Tr_{S^{3}}
. Then we can formulate the KK theory as follows, generalizing the results in
[
13
]

Proposition 6.1
.

The KK reduction of the Poisson BF theory on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the same as the holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory, associated to a
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet valued in the Lie algebra
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and a chiral multiplet valued in the adjoint representation

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),

(6.18)

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{w}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{*}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\bm{w}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{*}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{H}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2}).

The BV action functional consist of the standard twisted
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
action

\int\Tr_{S^{3}}\bm{w}(\hat{d}\mathbf{H}+\{\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H}\}_{\bar{\pi}})+\int\Tr_{S^{3}}\tilde{\bm{w}}(\hat{d}\tilde{\mathbf{H}}+\{\mathbf{H},\tilde{\mathbf{H}}\}_{\bar{\pi}}).

(6.19)

Together with the following deformations

\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\int\frac{1}{2(n-2)!}\Tr(\bm{w}\{\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H},\underbrace{\tilde{\bm{w}},\dots,\tilde{\bm{w}}}_{n-2}\}_{\bar{\pi},n})+\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\Tr(\tilde{\mathbf{H}}\{\mathbf{H},\underbrace{\tilde{\bm{w}},\dots,\tilde{\bm{w}}}_{n-1}\}_{\bar{\pi},n}).

(6.20)

6.3
The
L_{\infty}
structure on
T^{*}[1]\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})

In this section, we analyze the
L_{\infty}
structure on the CR cohomology
H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
induced from the Poisson bracket.

The 2-bracket on
H^{0,0}_{CR}(S^{3})=\mathbb{C}[w_{1},w_{2}]
is easy, because
w_{i}
is mapped to
z_{i}
under the isomorphism
K^{-1}
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\{w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q},w_{1}^{r}w_{1}^{s}\}_{\bar{\pi}}=\epsilon_{ij}(\partial_{w_{i}}w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q})(\partial_{w_{j}}w_{1}^{r}w_{1}^{s})

(6.21)

\displaystyle=(ps-qr)w_{1}^{p+r-1}w_{2}^{q+s-1}.

Therefore, we can identify
(H^{0,0}_{CR}(S^{3}),\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
with
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. The 2-bracket is also non zero on
H^{0,0}_{CR}(S^{3})\otimes H^{0,1}_{CR}(S^{3})
. To compute this, we recall that the isomorphism
K
maps
f(\bar{w}_{i})\epsilon
to
f(\frac{\bar{z}_{i}}{r^{2}})\frac{\bar{z}_{1}d\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{2}d\bar{z}_{1}}{r^{4}}
. So we first compute

\{z_{1}^{p}z_{2}^{q},\frac{\bar{z}_{1}^{r}\bar{z}_{2}^{s}\epsilon_{ij}\bar{z}_{i}d\bar{z}_{j}}{(z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{2})^{r+s+2}}\}_{\pi}=(r+s+2)(qz_{1}\bar{z}_{1}-pz_{2}\bar{z}_{2})\frac{z_{1}^{p-1}z_{2}^{q-1}\bar{z}_{1}^{r}\bar{z}_{1}^{s}\epsilon_{ij}\bar{z}_{i}d\bar{z}_{j}}{(z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{2})^{r+s+3}}.

(6.22)

Under the isomorphism
K
this is mapped to the element

(r+s+2)(qw_{1}\bar{w}_{1}-pw_{2}\bar{w}_{2})w_{1}^{p-1}w_{2}^{q-1}\bar{w}_{1}^{r}\bar{w}_{1}^{s}\epsilon.

(6.23)

This is an element in
\mathcal{H}_{\frac{p+q-1}{2},0}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0,\frac{r+s+1}{2}}\epsilon
. We need to compute its projection to
\mathcal{H}_{0,\frac{r+s-p-q}{2}+1}\epsilon
. This is analyzed in
4.18
, and we have the following

\{w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q},\frac{(r+s+1)!}{r!s!}\bar{w}_{1}^{r}\bar{w}_{1}^{s}\epsilon\}_{\bar{\pi}}=((r+1)q-(s+1)p)\frac{(r+s-p-q+3)!}{(r-p+1)!(s-q+1)!}\bar{w}_{1}^{r-p+1}\bar{w}_{2}^{s-q+1}\epsilon.

(6.24)

In fact, by identifying
\mathbb{C}[\bar{w}_{1},\bar{w}_{2}]\epsilon
with the (degree shifted) dual of
\mathbb{C}[w_{1},w_{2}]
, we can check that the above bracket can be identified with the bracket induced from the action of
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
on its dual. Therefore, the Lie algebra
(H^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}_{CR}(S^{3}),\{-,-\}_{\bar{\pi}})
is naturally identified with the Lie algebra
T^{*}[1]\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Higher brackets on
H^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}_{CR}(S^{3})
correspond to the
L_{\infty}
deformation of
T^{*}[1]\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. The method to compute the
L_{\infty}
structure is similar to the method to compute the
A_{\infty}
structure
4.3
. The difference is that we sum over maps constructed from binary rooted trees instead of binary planar rooted trees. For each rooted trees, we put the brackets on the vertices and
h
on the internal edges.

\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n}=\sum_{T\in BT_{n}}(\pm)\{...\}_{T}.

(6.25)

For example, the induced
3-
bracket is given by

\{a,b,c\}^{\bar{\pi}}_{3}=p\{a,h\{b,c\}\}-p\{h\{a,b\},c\}+p\{b,h\{c,a\}\}.

(6.26)

Using the bilinear pairing
\Tr_{S^{3}}
, the higher brackets satisfy

\Tr_{S^{3}}(a_{0}\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\}_{\bar{\pi},n})=(-1)^{n+|a_{n}|(|a_{0}|+\dots+|a_{n-1}|)}\Tr_{S^{3}}(a_{n}\{a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{1}}\}_{\bar{\pi},n}).

(6.27)

Therefore, for the
n
-th bracket
\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n}
, it suffice to compute it on the subspace
H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-1}
. For
a_{0}\in H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})
and
\bar{a}_{i}\epsilon\in H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})
, we have

\{a_{0},\bar{a}_{1}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{a}_{n-1}\epsilon\}_{\bar{\pi},n}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n-1}}p\{\dots h\{h\{a_{0},\bar{a}_{\sigma(1)}\epsilon\},\bar{a}_{\sigma(2)}\epsilon\},\dots,\bar{a}_{\sigma(n-1)}\epsilon\}.

(6.28)

We define the following constant

\displaystyle(\pi_{n})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,u_{n-1},v_{n-1}}:=

(6.29)

\displaystyle\prod_{i}\frac{(u_{i}+v_{i}+1)!}{u_{i}!v_{i}!}\times\Tr_{S^{3}}(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}p\{\dots h\{h\{w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s},\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{1}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{1}}\epsilon\},\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{2}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{2}}\epsilon\},\dots,\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{n-1}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{n-1}}\epsilon\}).

We have that
(\pi_{n})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,u_{n-1},v_{n-1}}
is non zero only when the following condition is hold

u_{1}+\dots+u_{n-1}=p+r-2n+3,\quad v_{1}+\dots+v_{n-1}=q+s-2n+3.

(6.30)

Then the
n
-th bracket
\{...\}_{\bar{\pi},n}
on the subspace
H_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes H_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})^{\otimes n-1}
can be expressed as follows

\displaystyle\prod_{i}\frac{(u_{i}+v_{i}+1)!}{u_{i}!v_{i}!}\{w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s},\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{1}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{1}}\epsilon,\dots,\bar{w}_{1}^{u_{n-1}}\bar{w}_{2}^{v_{n-1}}\epsilon\}_{\bar{\pi},n}=

(6.31)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n-1}}(\pi_{n})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{\sigma(1)},v_{\sigma(1)},\dots,u_{\sigma(n-1)},v_{\sigma(n-1)}}\right)\frac{(p+q+1)}{p!q!}\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon,

with
p
and
q
satisfying
6.30
.

This expression of the higher brackets also leads us to an expansion of the effective interaction of the KK theory of Poisson BF theory

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 3}\sum_{p,q,r,s\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1}+\dots u_{n-1}=p+r-2n+3\\
v_{1}+\dots v_{n-1}=q+s-2n+3\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n-1}}(\pi_{n})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{\sigma(1)},v_{\sigma(1)},\dots,u_{\sigma(n-1)},v_{\sigma(n-1)}}\right)

(6.32)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2(n-2)!}\int\mathbf{H}[p,q]\mathbf{H}[r,s]\mathbf{w}[u_{1},v_{1}]\tilde{\mathbf{w}}[u_{2},v_{2}]\dots\tilde{\mathbf{w}}[u_{n-1},v_{n-1}]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int\tilde{\mathbf{H}}[p,q]\mathbf{H}[r,s]\tilde{\mathbf{w}}[u_{1},v_{1}]\dots\tilde{\mathbf{w}}[u_{n-1},v_{n-1}]\right).

We provide an explicit expression for the constant
(\pi_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u_{1},v_{1},u_{2},v_{2}}
in
D.2
.

7
Kodaira Spencer gravity

7.1
A brief review

algebraic structure of Polyvector fields

The closed-string sector of the topological B-model can be described by a quantum field theory on space time called the Kodaira-Spencer theory of gravity
[
39
]
. This theory is generalized in
[
40
]
by turning on gravitational descendants and is called BCOV theory. To introduce this theory, we first introduce the space of polyvector fields on a Calabi-Yau three-fold
X
. Let

\mathrm{PV}^{j,i}(X)=\Omega^{0,i}(X,\wedge^{j}TX)

(7.1)

be the bundle of
(0,i)
forms with coefficients in the
j
-th exterior power of the holomorphic tangent bundle of
X
. We call them poly-vector fields on
X
. The anti-holomorphic Dolbeault differential
\bar{\partial}
on forms extended to polyvector fields and gives us a differential

\bar{\partial}:\mathrm{PV}^{j,i}(X)\to\mathrm{PV}^{j,i+1}(X).

(7.2)

Wedge product together with the differential
\bar{\partial}
makes
\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)
a differential graded commutative algebra.

Next we can identify
\mathrm{PV}^{j,i}(X)
with
\Omega^{3-j,i}
via contracting poly-vector fields with the holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{X}
on
X
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{PV}^{j,i}(X)

\displaystyle\cong\Omega^{3-j,i},

(7.3)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto\alpha\vee\Omega_{X}.

The differential
\partial
on forms
\Omega^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
defines an operator on
\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)
via the above isomorphism

\partial:\mathrm{PV}^{j,i}(X)\to\mathrm{PV}^{j-1,i}(X).

(7.4)

The
\partial
operator on polyvector fields is not a derivation with respect to the wedge product. The failure of it being a derivation is measured by the following bracket

\{\alpha,\beta\}:=\partial(\alpha\wedge\beta)-(\partial\alpha)\wedge\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\wedge(\partial\beta),

(7.5)

which coincides with the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket on polyvector fields (up to a sign). The fundamental algebraic structures of polyvector fields on Calabi-Yau geometry can be
summarized by saying that the tuple
\{\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X),\bar{\partial},\wedge,\partial,\{-,-\}\}
defines a differential graded Gerstenhaber-Batalin-Vilkovisky algebra. The BV theory of Kodaira-Spencer gravity, or BCOV theory, is built on this algebraic structure.

In a local coordinate, the algebra of polyvector fields can be written as

\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3})=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{3})[\eta^{i},d\bar{z}_{i}].

(7.6)

The differential
\partial
can be given by the expressions

\partial=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}.

(7.7)

The bracket can be given by the expression

\{\alpha,\beta\}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\alpha}{\partial\eta^{i}}\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial z_{i}}+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\frac{\partial\alpha}{\partial z_{i}}\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial\eta^{i}}.

(7.8)

Kodaira-Spencer theory

In the original formulation, Kodaira-Spencer theory has BV fields described by divergence free polyvector fields

\mu\in\ker\partial\subset\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)[2].

(7.9)

The Lagrangian takes the form

\frac{1}{2}\int_{X}(\partial^{-1}\mu)\wedge\bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{6}\int_{X}\mu\wedge\mu\wedge\mu.

(7.10)

The equation of motion can be obtained by varying the action functional, which is

\bar{\partial}\mu+\frac{1}{2}\{\mu,\mu\}=0.

(7.11)

One can see that the BV formalism of Kodaira-Spencer theory is encoded in the dg Lie algebra
(\ker\partial,\bar{\partial},\{-,-\})
, together with an ill-defined pairing
\int_{X}\partial^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}
. Though the kinetic term for Kodaira-Spencer theory is ill-defined, this theory has a well-defined propagator which allows one to analyze the theory in perturbation theory.

There is a consistent truncation of Kodaira-Spencer theory by simply dropping the fields in
\mathrm{PV}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
and
\mathrm{PV}^{2,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
. We are left with fields in
\ker\partial\subset\mathrm{PV}^{1,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)[2]
and action functional of the same form as
7.10
. This theory is called the
(1,0)
Kodaira-Spencer theory, or type I Kodaira Spencer gravity because its relationship to the ordinary B-model is similar to the relationship of physical type I string theory to the IIB string theory
[
41
]
. It is a pleasant feature shown in
[
7
]
that the full Kodaira-Spencer theory is equivalent to an Abelian Chern-Simons theory valued in
\Pi\mathbb{C}^{2}
coupled with the
(1,0)
Kodaira-Spencer theory. Therefore, for the analysis of KK reduction of Kodaira-Spencer theory, we only need to focus on the fields in
\mathrm{PV}^{1,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)
.

In
[
40
]
, a different formulation of Kodaira-Spencer theory, called BCOV theory, is proposed, where the constraint
\partial\mu=0
is imposed homologically. This theory produces a better behaved BV theory and admits rigorous perturbation quantization
[
40
]
. However, in this paper, we still proceed with the old formulation of Kodaira-Spencer gravity for simplicity.

7.2
Some useful definitions

The space of Polyvector fields on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
is given by

\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C})[\partial_{z},\partial_{z_{1}},\partial_{z_{2}},d\bar{z},d\bar{z}_{1},d\bar{z}_{2}].

(7.12)

We extend the isomorphism
3.1
to an isomorphism

K:\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C})\to\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})[\partial_{z},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}],

(7.13)

where we simply maps
\partial_{z_{i}}
to
\eta_{i}
. Using this isomorphism, the BV algebra structure on polyvector fields induces a BV algebra structure on
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})[\partial_{z},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
. For example, the BV operator is defined by
K\circ\partial\circ K^{-1}
.

By identifying the space of polyvector fields
\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
with
\Omega^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}_{Dol}
using the volume form, we can further identify the above complex with
\Omega^{(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}),\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}_{3d}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. However, it will be more convenient to use the space
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0})\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})[\partial_{z},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
when we deal with the open-closed coupling in later sections.

We are interested in the
SU(2)
decomposition of the above complex. For this purpose, we construct the following two
SU(2)
invariant vector fields

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\bar{w}_{2}\eta_{1}-\bar{w}_{1}\eta_{2},

(7.14)

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}

\displaystyle=w_{1}\eta_{1}+w_{2}\eta_{2}.

Note that
\eta
is the basis of the bundle
T^{\mathbb{C}}S^{3}\cap T^{(1,0)}\mathbb{C}^{2}
where
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the ambient space of
S^{3}
. The vector fields
\partial_{z}
,
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
generate the whole polyvector fields because

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=w_{2}\eta+\bar{w}_{1}\hat{\eta},

(7.15)

\displaystyle\eta_{2}

\displaystyle=-w_{1}\eta-\bar{w}_{2}\hat{\eta},

and

\eta\wedge\hat{\eta}=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}.

(7.16)

The vector fields
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
act on the complex
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
via the Schouten bracket. These actions are important because they determine the interactions of the Kodaira-Spencer theory and its coupling with the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory.

First, we analyze the action of the two vector fields
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. This can be obtained from the
SU(2)
decomposition of
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. Since both vector fields are
SU(2)
invariant, on each irreducible subspace they act as either zero or scalar multiples of the identity onto an irreducible subspace of the same
SU(2)
representation.
For
\eta
, we have

\eta:\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{H}_{0,\bar{j}}\to 0\\
\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathcal{H}_{j-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}.

(7.17)

The action of
\eta
on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
is completely characterized by its action on the highest weight vector of each space
\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}

\eta(w_{1}^{2j}\bar{w}_{2}^{2\bar{j}})=2jw_{1}^{2j-1}\bar{w}_{2}^{2\bar{j}+1}.

(7.18)

Using this vector
\eta
we can define another action on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
by
\partial(-\wedge\eta)
. We can check that
\partial(-\wedge\eta)=\eta(-)
.

We see that the operator
\eta
has a very similar behavior as the homotopy operator
h
defined in Section
4.1
except that
\eta
is of degree
0
. It is useful to define a degree zero operator
\tilde{h}:\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\to\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}
as follows

\tilde{h}(Y)=h(Y\epsilon),\quad\text{for any }Y\in\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}.

(7.19)

Then the operator
\eta
and
\tilde{h}
are related as follows

\eta|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}=(2j)(2\bar{j}+1)\tilde{h}|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}.

(7.20)

This relation will be useful later. We can use it to reduce computation in Kodaira-Spencer theory into computation in holomorphic Chern-Simons theory.

Now we consider the other
SU(2)
invariant vector
\hat{\eta}
. The action of
\hat{\eta}
on
\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})
is given by

\hat{\eta}:\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{H}_{j,j}\to 0\\
\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\quad\text{ for }j\neq\bar{j}\end{array}.

(7.21)

It act on the highest weight vector of
\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}
by the constant
\hat{\eta}(w_{1}^{2j}\bar{w}_{2}^{2\bar{j}})=(2j-2\bar{j})w_{1}^{2j}\bar{w}_{2}^{2\bar{j}}
. Therefore we have

\hat{\eta}|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}=(2j-2\bar{j})\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}.

(7.22)

We can also consider the operation defined by
\partial(-\wedge\hat{\eta})
. Unlike
\eta
, the operations
\partial(-\wedge\hat{\eta})\neq\hat{\eta}(-)
. We find that

\displaystyle\partial(-\wedge\hat{\eta})|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}=(2j-2\bar{j}+2)\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}},

(7.23)

\displaystyle\partial(-\wedge\hat{\eta})|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\epsilon}=(2j-2\bar{j})\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}.

In fact, the action of
\hat{\eta}
on
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
is also nontrivial. For example,
\hat{\eta}(t)
is computed by
(z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}}+z_{2}\partial_{z_{2}})(r)=\frac{1}{2}r
. We have

\hat{\eta}(f(t))=\frac{1}{2}t\partial_{t}f(t).

(7.24)

This means that
\hat{\eta}
will have a nontrivial action on the topological
t
direction. Terms like this will be annoying in our later formulation of the KK theory. However, we can proceed as we deal with the Poisson BF theory. We first pass to the
\hat{d}
cohomology without introducing any higher product. By passing to the
\hat{d}
cohomology, the action of
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}
is trivial. As a consequence, we can forget the action of
\eta
and
\hat{\eta}
on
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
and only consider their action on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
. This greatly simplifies our later analysis of the KK theory.

7.3
KK reduction

It was shown in
[
7
]
that the
PV^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
and
PV^{2,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
part of Kodaira-Spencer fields can be reformulated as a (super) abelian holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. So it suffices to focus on the
PV^{1,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
fields. Kodaira-Spencer theory is more subtle than our previous examples because fields are subject to the constraint
\partial\bm{\mu}=0
. This equation cut out a subspace of
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\partial_{z}\oplus\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\eta\oplus\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\hat{\eta}
that forms the tower of KK fields. One can proceed as in our previous examples. The Dolbeault differential and the Schouten bracket of polyvector fields defined a
L_{\infty}
structure on the cohomology of the subspace subject to the
\partial
constraint. However, this approach will end up with cumbersome notation. To simplify the discussion, we directly write down the KK fields that solve the
\partial
constraint. We find the following fields

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}=

\displaystyle\sum_{p+q\geq 1}\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}(\bm{\beta}[p,q]\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon\partial_{z}+\frac{1}{p+q}\partial_{z}\bm{\beta}[p,q]\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon\hat{\eta}),

(7.25)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}=

\displaystyle(p+q)\bm{\alpha}[p,q]w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\eta-\sum_{p+q\geq 1}\bm{\alpha}[p,q]\eta(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q})\hat{\eta}.

Note that the KK tower start from the modes
p+q=1
.

We can check that both of the fields are in the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology and the kernel of
\partial
. The corresponding action functional can be obtained from the Kodaira-Spencer action functional as follows

\int\Tr_{S^{3}}\left((\partial^{-1}\bm{\beta})\hat{d}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\beta}\bm{\alpha}\bm{\alpha}\right).

(7.26)

With the help of the decomposition into KK towers, we can explicit compute the
\partial^{-1}
operation on the fields:

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}\bm{\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}(\frac{1}{p+q}\bm{\beta}[p,q]\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon\partial_{z}\wedge\hat{\eta}),

(7.27)

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}\bm{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}[p,q]w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\eta\wedge\hat{\eta}.

This feature displays the major advantage of working with the KK theory. The KK theory utilizes the
SU(2)
action on fields that allows us to solve the
\partial^{-1}
operation, which is annoying in the analysis of the original
6d
theory. We can expand the action functional explicitly in terms of the KK modes as follows

\sum\int\bm{\beta}[p,q]\hat{d}\bm{\alpha}[p,q]+\sum(ps-qr)\int\bm{\alpha}[p,q]\bm{\alpha}[r,s]\bm{\beta}[p+r-1,q+s-1].

(7.28)

The coefficient in front of the quadratic term is computed by

\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}\Tr_{S^{3}}(\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q})=1.

(7.29)

The coefficient in front of the cubic term is computed by

\displaystyle\frac{(p+q+s+r-1)!}{(p+r-1)!(s+m-1)!}\Tr_{S^{3}}(\eta(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q})\hat{\eta}(w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s})\bar{w}_{1}^{p+r-1}\bar{w}_{2}^{q+s-1})-(p\leftrightarrow rq\leftrightarrow s)

(7.30)

\displaystyle=(ps-qr).

We see that the action functional takes the same form as the
3d
holomorphic topological theory. As we will see later, this feature allows us to easily compute the boundary chiral algebra.

The coefficient in front of the cubic term is the same as the structure constant of the Lie algebra
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Therefore, this part of Kodaira-Spencer theory is the same as the holomorphic twist of
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory associated to a vector multiplet valued in
\mathrm{Ham}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Of course, higher structures exist and should give rise to higher order interaction terms. We briefly discuss them in the next section.

Similarly, we have the following fields

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\beta}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{p,q\geq 0}\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}[p,q]\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon\eta,

(7.31)

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{p,q\geq 0}\partial_{z}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[p,q]w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\hat{\eta}-(p+q+2)\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[p,q]w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\partial_{z}.

This KK tower starts from the modes
p,q=0
. The corresponding action functional is given by

\int(\partial^{-1}\tilde{\bm{\beta}})\hat{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}+\tilde{\bm{\beta}}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}.

(7.32)

We can compute the
\partial^{-1}
operation on the fields:

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}=

\displaystyle\frac{(p+q+1)!}{p!q!}(\frac{1}{p+q+2}\tilde{\bm{\beta}}[p,q]\bar{w}_{1}^{p}\bar{w}_{2}^{q}\epsilon\eta\wedge\hat{\eta}),

(7.33)

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[p,q]w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\partial_{z}\wedge\hat{\eta}.

This allows us to expand the action functional explicitly as follows

\displaystyle\sum\int\tilde{\bm{\beta}}[p,q]\hat{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[p,q]+(p+q+2)\int\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\bm{\alpha}[p,q]\partial_{z}\bm{\alpha}[r,s]

(7.34)

\displaystyle-(r+s+2)\int\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\partial_{z}\bm{\alpha}[p,q]\bm{\alpha}[r,s].

Due to the
\partial_{z}
that is present in the action functional, this theory does not come from any
3d

\mathcal{N}=2
theory. However, it still shares many similar features with the holomorphic twisted theory and can be analyzed in a similar fashion.

There are also coupling between the two part of Kodaira-Spencer fields, given as follows

\int\Tr_{S^{3}}\bm{\beta}\bm{\alpha}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}+\tilde{\bm{\beta}}\bm{\alpha}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}.

(7.35)

We emphasize that the subspace of
PV^{1,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
cut out by the constraint
\partial\bm{\mu}=0
does not have a canonical choice of basis. In other words, the KK theory of Kodaria-Spencer gravity can be formulated by different representatives of KK fields that have different action functional. The different choices of basis should be related by a recombination of the KK modes. We made our above choice to make the boundary chiral algebra compatible with the celestial chiral algebra studied in
[
13
]
.

7.4
Higher order interaction

Like our previous examples, higher order interactions for the KK theory of Kodaira-Spencer gravity exist and can be obtained from homotopy transfer. However, we need to be careful here because the homotopy operator in this case is not the homotopy operator
h
defined in Section
4.1
anymore. The reason for this is that the CR differential takes a slightly different form on
\mathrm{PV}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}},\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}
under the basis
\eta,\hat{\eta}
. We can compute the following

\bar{\partial}_{b}(\partial_{z})=\bar{\partial}_{b}(\hat{\eta})=0,\;\;\bar{\partial}_{b}(\eta)=\epsilon\hat{\eta}.

(7.36)

This implies that the CR differential
\bar{\partial}_{b}
act on
\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})\eta
as follows

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{b}:\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\eta

\displaystyle\to\begin{array}[]{l l}\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\epsilon\hat{\eta}&\bar{j}=0,\\
\mathcal{H}_{j+\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}-\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon\eta\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\epsilon\hat{\eta}&\bar{j}\geq\frac{1}{2},\end{array}

(7.37)

\displaystyle Y\eta

\displaystyle\mapsto\bar{\partial}_{b}(Y)\eta+Y\epsilon\hat{\eta}.

Accordingly, we get a new homotopy operator
\mathds{h}
. Since
\bar{\partial}_{b}
act on
\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\partial_{z}
and
\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\hat{\eta}
in the same way as it act on
\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})
, the new homotopy operator
\mathds{h}
acts on
\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\partial_{z}
and
\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\hat{\eta}
in the same way as
h
act on them. The only difference is the action of
\mathds{h}
on
\Omega_{b}^{0,1}(S^{3})\eta
, given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathds{h}:\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\epsilon\eta

\displaystyle\to\begin{array}[]{l l}0&j=0,\\
\mathcal{H}_{j-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon\eta&j=\frac{1}{2},\\
\mathcal{H}_{j-\frac{1}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon\eta\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j-1,\bar{j}+1}\epsilon\hat{\eta}&j\geq 1,\end{array}

(7.38)

\displaystyle Y\epsilon\eta

\displaystyle\mapsto\tilde{h}(Y)\eta-\tilde{h}^{2}(Y)\hat{\eta}.

Then higher order interaction of the KK theory is computed by terms like

\int\partial^{-1}\bm{\beta}\{\bm{\alpha},\mathds{h}\{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta}\}\}+\dots

(7.39)

We leave it to future work to study these terms in detail.

7.5
Coupling to holomorphic Chern-Simons theory

BCOV theory can be coupled to the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. The coupled theory has a unique quantization
[
17
]
and gives us a concrete realization of open-closed string field theory. In this paper, we are interested in the leading order coupling between the Kodaria spencer fields and the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. In particular, the coupling between an elements
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}^{i}\partial_{z_{i}}\in\mathrm{PV}^{1,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(X)\cap\ker\partial
and the holomorphic Chern-Simons is given by

\frac{1}{2}\int\Omega_{X}\Tr(\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}\wedge\bm{\mu}^{i}(\partial_{z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}})).

(7.40)

We are interested in the coupling after the KK reduction. If we substitute
\bm{\mu}
with
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\beta}
in the above coupling, we get the following terms

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\Tr(\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q]\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[r,s])+\frac{1}{2}\frac{r+s}{p+q+r+s}\int\Tr(\partial_{z}\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q]\mathbf{A}[r,s])

(7.41)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(ps-qr)\int\Tr(\bm{\alpha}[r,s]\mathbf{B}[p+r-1,q+s-1]\mathbf{A}[p,q])

If we substitute
\bm{\mu}
with
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
and
\tilde{\bm{\beta}}
, we get the following terms

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(ps-qr)}{p+q+r+s}\int\Tr(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}[p+r-1,q+s-1]\mathbf{A}[p,q]\mathbf{A}[r,s])

(7.42)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(p+q)\int\Tr(\partial_{z}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[r,s]\mathbf{B}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q])-\frac{1}{2}(r+s+2)\int\Tr(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[r,s]\mathbf{B}[p+r,q+s]\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[p,q]).

Just like our previous examples, there are higher order interactions coming from homotopy transfer.
6
6

6

There are also higher order couplings between BCOV theory and holomorphic Chern-Simons theory. They should also appear in the KK theory but should be distinguished from the higher order interaction discussed in this section that comes from the homotopy transfer of leading order coupling.
They can be computed by the same method of summing over trees as before, replacing the
2
-brackets by the action of Kodaira-Spencer fields on the Chern-Simons form. The simplest one is the following

I_{OC}=\int\mathbf{B}[\mathbf{A},h(\bm{\beta}\mathbf{A})].

(7.43)

Using the expansion
7.25
of the Kodaira-Spencer fields, we have

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+\tilde{u}=p+r-1\\
v+\tilde{v}=q+s-1\end{subarray}}(m_{3})^{p,q;r,s}_{u,v}\int\Tr\mathbf{B}[\tilde{u},\tilde{v}]\left[\mathbf{A}[p,q],\left(\bm{\beta}[u,v]\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[r,s]+\partial_{z}\bm{\beta}[u,v]\frac{r+s}{u+v}\mathbf{A}[r,s]\right)\right]

(7.44)

\displaystyle+(m_{3})^{r,s;p,q}_{u,v}\int\Tr\mathbf{B}[\tilde{u},\tilde{v}]\left[\left(\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[p,q]\bm{\beta}[u,v]+\frac{p+q}{u+v}\mathbf{A}[p,q]\partial_{z}\bm{\beta}[u,v]\right),\mathbf{A}[r,s]\right].

There are also coupling of the form
\int\mathbf{B}[\mathbf{A},h(\bm{\alpha}\mathbf{B})]+\dots
. Finding their explicit expansion requires us to compute expression of the form
pM(\eta(w_{1}^{r}w_{2}^{s}),\hat{\eta}hM(w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q},\bar{w}_{1}^{u}\bar{w}_{2}^{v}\epsilon))
. We leave them to future work.

7.6
Coupling to holomorphic BF theory

Kodaria-Spencer gravity can also be coupled to the holomorphic BF theory. The coupling takes the same form as the coupling with holomorphic Chern-Simons theory

\frac{1}{2}\int\Omega_{X}\Tr(\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}\wedge\bm{\mu}^{i}(\partial_{z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}})).

(7.45)

The only difference is that
\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}
is the field of holomorphic BF theory now. According to
5.2
, after KK reduction, the field
\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}
decompose into KK fields
\mathbf{A}[p,q]
and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}[p,q]
. Since the coupling takes the same form as in the last section, it is straightforward to write down the first-order coupling of the KK fields. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\Tr(\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q]\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[r,s])+\frac{1}{2}\frac{r+s}{p+q+r+s}\int\Tr(\partial_{z}\bm{\beta}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q]\mathbf{A}[r,s])

(7.46)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(ps-qr)\int\Tr(\bm{\alpha}[r,s]\tilde{\mathbf{B}}[p+r-1,q+s-1]\mathbf{A}[p,q]).

Similarly, we have the coupling with the
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}},\tilde{\bm{\beta}}
fields

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(ps-qr)}{p+q+r+s}\int\Tr(\tilde{\bm{\beta}}[p+r-1,q+s-1]\mathbf{A}[r,s]\mathbf{A}[p,q])

(7.47)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(p+q)\int\Tr(\partial_{z}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[r,s]\tilde{\mathbf{B}}[p+r,q+s]\mathbf{A}[p,q])-\frac{1}{2}(r+s+2)\int\Tr(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}[r,s]\tilde{\mathbf{B}}[p+r,q+s]\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[p,q]).

Higher order couplings also exists and we leave them to future work.

8
Theories in deformed geometry

8.1
The deformed geoemtry

In the twisted holography setup, we place a stack of
N
topological B-branes wrapping the complex line
\mathbb{C}=\{z_{i}=0\}
in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. This brane configuration sources non-trivial flux and induces gravitational backreaction. The backreaction of such a stack of branes is analyzed in
[
7
]
. We briefly review the results here.

Solving the equation of motion of Kodaira-Spencer gravity with a localized source on the brane gives us the following Beltrami differential

\beta_{0}=\frac{2N}{8\pi^{2}}\frac{\bar{z}_{1}d\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{2}d\bar{z}_{1}}{(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}\partial_{z}.

(8.1)

This Beltrami differential defines a new complex structure on
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
. Holomorphic functions of this new complex structure satisfy

(\bar{\partial}+\beta)F=0,\;

(8.2)

where
\beta
act on
F
as a vector field. Solving this equation, we obtained the new holomorphic coordinates, which are
z_{1},z_{2}
and

\displaystyle u_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{1}z-\frac{N\bar{z}_{2}}{|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}},

(8.3)

\displaystyle u_{2}

\displaystyle=z_{2}z+\frac{N\bar{z}_{1}}{|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}}.

These coordinates are not independent. They satisfy

u_{1}z_{1}-u_{1}z_{2}=N.

(8.4)

This is the equation defining the deformed conifold
X_{N}
as a submanifold of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. If we collect the coordinates into a single matrix

g=\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&z_{2}\\
u_{1}&u_{2}.\end{pmatrix}

(8.5)

Then the defining equation of the deformed conifold becomes

\det g=N.

(8.6)

Therefore the deformed geometry is also equivalent to
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

8.2
Holomorphic Chern-Simons in deformed geometry, I

In this section, we analyze the KK theory of holomorphic Chern-Simons in the deformed geometry. One way to understand the effect of the new geometry on the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory is through the open-closed coupling. Note that new complex structure of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is completely encoded in the Beltrami differential
\beta_{0}=\frac{2N}{8\pi^{2}}\frac{\bar{z}_{1}d\bar{z}_{2}-\bar{z}_{2}d\bar{z}_{1}}{(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}\partial_{z}
, which can be regarded as a background value of the dynamical closed string fields. The interaction of complex structure moduli with the open string field is described by the coupling introduced in Section
7.5

\int\Omega_{X}\Tr(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}}\wedge\bm{\mu}^{i}(\partial_{z_{i}}\boldsymbol{\EuScript{A}})).

(8.7)

Therefore, working in the new geometry is equivalent to turning on a background Beltrami differential
\beta_{0}
in the above open-closed coupling.

Via the coordinate transform
3.1
we rewrite the Beltrami differential
\beta_{0}
as
N\epsilon\partial_{z}
, where we absorbed a constant factor which can be restored from a rescaling. We see that by turning on this background Beltrami differential, the open-closed coupling term gives us the following:

N\int\Tr(\bm{\mathcal{A}}\wedge\epsilon\partial_{z}\bm{\mathcal{A}}),

(8.8)

where
\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,0}(S^{3})\otimes\mathfrak{g}
is the KK fields of holomorphic Chern-Simons theory before we pass to the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology. This term describes the deformed action functional for holomorphic Chern-Simons theory in the
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
geometry.

We would like to understand the corresponding deformation for the KK theory after we pass to the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology. A naive answer is that we simply restrict the above term to the fields
\mathbf{A}
. This will introduce a term
N\int\Tr(\mathbf{A}[0,0]\epsilon\partial_{z}\mathbf{A}[0,0])
, which is a Chern-Simons deformation to the zero KK modes. This is compatible with the observation in
[
7
]
. However, by integrating out massive fields, there are higher order corrections to this zero mode answer.

We can see this by analyzing the complex of KK fields. Note that adding the deformation
8.8
is equivalent to add the differential
N\epsilon\partial_{z}
to the complex
(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+\bar{\partial}_{b})
. Therefore, the new theory on deformed geometry is described by the following complex

(\Omega_{3d}\otimes\Omega_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+\bar{\partial}_{b}+N\epsilon\partial_{z}).

(8.9)

Due to the presence of the term
N\epsilon\partial_{z}
, new differential will be generated after we take the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
cohomology of the above complex. It will be a laborious work to follow the standard procedure of spectral sequence to obtain the right answer. Here, we use the homological perturbation lemma (we provide more details of homological perturbation lemma in
C.4
), which gives us the correct differential on
\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3})
to make it quasi-isomorphic to the above complex
8.9
. We have

(\Omega_{3d}^{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}\otimes H_{b}^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(S^{3}),\hat{d}+D),

(8.10)

where the differential
D
is obtained by homological perturbation lemma, given by the following formula

D=(1-N\epsilon\partial_{z}h)^{-1}\epsilon\partial_{z}=N\epsilon\partial_{z}+N^{2}\epsilon\partial_{z}h\epsilon\partial_{z}+\dots

(8.11)

Using the degree
0
operator
\tilde{h}
defined in
7.19
, the above expression can be simplified to

D=\sum_{k\geq 1}N^{k}\epsilon\tilde{h}^{k-1}\partial_{z}^{k}.

(8.12)

Given this new differential, we find the following deformed action functional

I^{(1)}_{O}=\int\frac{1}{2}\Tr(\mathbf{A}D\mathbf{A})=\sum_{k\geq 1}I^{(1,k)}_{O},

(8.13)

where

I^{(1,k)}_{O}=\frac{1}{2}N^{k}\int\Tr(\mathbf{A}\epsilon\tilde{h}^{k-1}\partial_{z}^{k}\mathbf{A}).

(8.14)

We can simplify the above expression by analyzing the operator
\tilde{h}^{k}
. Note that the action of
\tilde{h}^{k}
takes the following form

\tilde{h}^{k}:\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\to 0,\\
\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}\overset{\simeq}{\to}\mathcal{H}_{j-\frac{k}{2},\bar{j}+\frac{k}{2}},\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\text{ for }j<\frac{k}{2},\\
\text{ for }j\geq\frac{k}{2}.\end{array}

(8.15)

When non-zero,
\tilde{h}^{k}
acts as the following constant

\tilde{h}^{k}|_{\mathcal{H}_{j,\bar{j}}}=\sqrt{\frac{(2j-k)!(2\bar{j})!}{(2j)!(2\bar{j}+k)!}}.

(8.16)

We are interested in the action of
\tilde{h}^{k}
on
\mathcal{H}_{k,0}
. It is easy to find that

\tilde{h}^{k}(e^{(k)}_{i})=\frac{1}{k!}\bar{e}^{(k)}_{i}.

(8.17)

Transform this result into the unnormalized basis
\{w_{1}^{p}w_{2}^{q}\}
, we obtain

I^{(1,k)}_{O}=\sum_{p+q=k-1}N^{k}\frac{(-1)^{p}}{(k-1)!}\frac{p!q!}{k!}\frac{1}{2}\int\Tr(\mathbf{A}[p,q]dz\partial_{z}^{k}\mathbf{A}[q,p]).

(8.18)

These interaction terms describe part of the deformation of the KK theory of holomorphic Chern-Simons in the deformed geometry.

8.3
An alternative approach

In this section, we briefly comment on performing the KK reduction directly on the deformed conifold. The deformed conifold has a
SU(2)_{R}
symmetry by matrix multiplication from the right. The quotient

SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})/SU(2)_{R}

(8.19)

is precisely the hyperbolic space – the Euclidean version of
AdS_{3}
.

The quotient space is parameterized by coordinates
\rho=gg^{\dagger}
. We have

\rho=\begin{pmatrix}r^{2}&\bar{z}r^{2}\\
zr^{2}&\frac{N^{2}}{16\pi^{4}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}+|z|^{2}r^{2}\end{pmatrix},

(8.20)

where
r=(z_{1}\bar{z}_{1}+z_{2}\bar{z}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Therefore, we can also use the coordinates
(z,\bar{z},r)
for the quotient.

KK reduction of field theory on
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
amounts to analyzing the Dolbeault complex

(\Omega^{0,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}}}}(SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})),\bar{\partial}).

(8.21)

Recall that
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is obtained as a deformation of
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
. The spaces of functions (not necessarily holomorphic) on them and the decomposition of the Dolbeault complexes under the
SU(2)_{R}
action are the same. The only difference is their complex structure, encoded in the Dolbeault differential
\bar{\partial}
. Note that the new complex structure of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
can be obtained by adding the Beltrami differential
8.1
to the Dolbeault differential
\bar{\partial}
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
. Therefore, the Dolbeault complex on
SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is equivalent to the complex obtained by adding the Beltrami differential
8.1
to the Dolbeault complex
3.10
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}\backslash\mathbb{C}
